ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e139	Honourable co-representatives! Former senior representative Kristin Kverneland Wohningdal is dead, 87 years old. Kristin Wohnningdal was born on 1 January 1923 in Stavanger. She graduated artium in 1942 and pharmacies degree in 1947. She was a trained pharmacist and work at the pharmacy in Risør, Tvedestrand and Stavanger. In 1963, Kristin Wohnningdal was elected to Stavanger City Council and chairmanship of the Right. Until 1973 she had a number of trusts in both public and political bodies. She was a member of the County Council and County Committee, and from 1969 to 1974 she was a member of the Right Central Government. She was also a member of the Departmental Board of the House Bank of the West and an alternate member of the Board of Governors of Statoil. Kristin Lönningdal was alternate to the Stortinget for Rogaland for two periods, from 1965 to 1973. In the fall of 1973, she was elected as a permanent representative of the Right. She was re-elected in 1977, but she made re-elections at the 1981 Grand Prix. In the first period, she was a member of the Municipal and Environmental Protection Committee and subsequently of the Financial Committee. During the time she was a permanent representative of the Stortinget, she was delegated to the UN General Assembly and a member of the board of the Stortinget Group of the Interparliamentary Union. Kristin Lönningdal was secretary of state in Odelstinget from 1977 until she was elected vice-president in the same thing in the fall of 1980. Kristin Lönningdal was appointed County of Rogaland in 1981 and held this office for ten years. Kristin Wohnningdal's commitment to Stortinget was far-fetched. She was interested in many tasks – from housing and hospital policy to environmental protection and economic policy. She was concerned about the individual's right and of helping those who felt that they were coming to a brief moment in the meeting with public bureaucracy. These were questions that she was deeply concerned about in her actions as a countyman. Kristin Wohnningdal had a clear profile. She was always right on the matter and asserted her opinions clearly and unequivocally, while she was constantly emboldened by a friendly spirit towards colleagues and employees. We remember Kristin Kverneland Wage Valley for her work in the Storting and shines peace over her memory. The representatives heard the President's commemorative speech.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e230	As the President said, we have, for consideration, proposed 14 S by Trine Skei Grande and Borghild Tenden, on behalf of the Left. This is a proposal with almost the same wording as previous proposals, which have been examined on three occasions, in 2000–2001, 2003–04 and 2007–08. As in previous readings, the Presidency proposes that the proposal be rejected. The background, and the rationale, for it is very much the same as in previous discussions on similar proposals, namely that we perceive it as a quality at Norwegian level that it is very open and accessible, and that it is easy for Norwegian citizens to get in touch with the representatives of Storting. We still think that should be the situation. And as for the specific content of point 1 of the proposal, which concerns Stortinget, it would not have been a register just for professional lobbyists, but a register of any person who wants to contact a depositor. That is what we believe will reduce the quality of democracy, that is, the ease of access to the Biggest. This time we have had a relatively thorough review of the different forms of lobbying in other countries and found that there is little experience to gain from the approach of the proposal, namely by simply having a register of arrival in Stortinget. For example, a system has been set up in the European Parliament to register as a lobbyist and thus gain access to the European Parliament, but it is therefore a completely different solution to what is proposed in the proposal for a representative. This does not mean that the presidency does not seem to think that many of the implications of lobbying are worth discussing – and there has been a good and broad discussion about it in the last few months. It itself contributes to awareness of who influences the political processes. We therefore believe that there is an important debate, but to a large extent a debate different from that addressed in the proposed representative. We know, for example. that many of the activities of professional PR agencies would just not be covered by a Stortingen register, because it takes place to a very large extent either by meeting the representatives of the Stortingen outside the Stortinge or, perhaps mainly, by being an adviser to those who meet in the Stortinge – but not even meet in the Stortinge – for example. in the context of consultations. Nevertheless, we think it is important to have a live discussion about it, and here I believe we have something to learn from other countries. For example, for the type of arrangements that exist substantially in other countries, we are. have been introduced by decision in 2005, concerning the quarantine of policy-makers either from the type of public relations agency or as per. definition has unknown customer mass, or for the sake of those who leave the policy and enter the type of public relations activity. The intention of the 2005 decision was to organise this in a good way. This has now been practised since 2005, with discussions, for example: about who is being treated in the Quarantine Committee, who is receiving quarantine, and not least who is not receiving quarantine, and the difference between those receiving quarantine and those who are being banned. The Presidency believes it is important to look at all of these things in the light of the experience we have. That's why we're asking the government to provide an assessment of the experience with the quarantine regulations. I would like to stress that in the public debate on this issue, it has been claimed that quarantine rules prevent the transition from policy to private business and, in particular, from private business to politics. I am concerned that we should include representatives of private business in Norway in the policy as well. But it's not like a consulting industry that has hidden the mass of members, and that aims to influence decisions in the big business and government, is the same thing as any private sector job in a company where you know who you're working for. Something special about the industry – and then I think about the professional part of the industry – is that you have hidden customer mass. A minority in the Presidency might say something about part of the setting that is about that. At the same time, there are many, for example: Some lawyers, who provide advice on how to communicate with and influence public authorities, and they have the legal protection of the right – they are actually required – to keep hidden who are their clients. So there's a lot of dilemmas about this question. Then there is another theme that has been central to the debate, and on which the Presidency has also spent time. This is the question of access to Stortinget by former staging representatives. It is true that some former representatives of big business also help to guide how to deal with the public and political decision-making process. It was clarified, or decided, during the previous ageing period that former staging representatives could not bring others in. So you can't be a host in Stortinget based on your access card to Stortinget. I think that was an important clarification, because it has also happened that the representatives of Storting may be somewhat uncertain whether former colleagues are here professionally or whether they are here as former colleagues. The Presidency is prepared to make this very clear, of course, that the invitation to have a sign of access to the Stortinget is to be able to visit the Stortinget and former colleagues as a former representative, not intended to be a sign of access used in another professional context. So the Presidency is ready to make this clear. And then I'll finally be allowed to say that there's been a lot of discussions about people who come from the PR industry, people who go to the PR industry, and in some cases also representatives who work in the PR industry. It's also the Presidency's view that it's not all things we can manage to regulate by law. So at the end of the day, the representatives of the Storting are not employed by the Presidency. They're actually not employed by anyone. Nor can the Presidency ask someone who is an alternate to the Stortinget, to quit the job he has, to start another job. Ultimately, this is also about the parties being aware of how they deal with their candidates, and how parties and candidates are assessed by their voters. Some of this discretion will also have to lie in the very essence of democracy in the future. It is a matter of trust, and to a large extent it is a question of the responsibility of the parties for maintaining that trust. On that basis, I would recommend that Stortinget adopt the current state of affairs of the Presidency.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e287	Open influence is an entirely legitimate part of Norwegian democracy. It is – and should be – a high door and a wide gate for people who want to visit the Stortinge to present their case and their arguments. The democratic problems arise only when the attempts to influence paid information advisors are carried out in secret. While traditional lobbying is characterised by professional lobbyists promoting the case on behalf of others, the situation in Norway is that companies engaged in information consulting act as invisible backers of the sponsors, who themselves have contact with politicians. The left's proposal is directed towards traditional lobbying, and although such a lobbying register is a good idea and SV supports the intentions behind the proposal, a lobbying register alone will not solve the problem we're looking to solve. Firstly, a lobby register could quickly mean that the contact between Stortinget and the lobbyists is moved out of Stortinget, and then we are equally far, even though I had hoped – and I am almost sure – that the staging representatives respect the laws they themselves adopt. But as others before me have said, the father is present so that such a law can still be circumvented. Secondly, the main problem today is not that Stortinget is run down by professional lobbyists. Rather, I would like to say that there could have been more people coming to the Stortinget and telling them what they thought about politics, what they thought was important to Norway, which way they think development should take. These include: therefore SV has completed many lobby courses for partner organisations, to learn the tricks free of charge, the tricks for which others have to pay dearly. That's why SV has also arranged their own recording rounds for the state budget, at the latest this winter, as we call 'Gi SV message', how many small – known and unknown – stakeholder organisations get to come and meet big business representatives and make recordings to next year's government budget. What is important in this matter is what we call, in other contexts – if the President allows it – ‘professional backers’. What has made this matter up to date is that well-known politicians, such as former Wraith representative Carl I. Hagen and former representative and councillor Bjarne Håkon Hanssen, have been employed in professional advisory companies that hire out their services, including lobbyists, for good money to the companies and the interests that can afford to pay for them. That's where the problems arise, when professional backers sell their advice on how to win in democracy, for several thousand dollars an hour, to those who can afford to buy these advices. Then the influence is no longer open and direct, but indirect and hidden. They're the ones with the most money that can afford to buy the most expensive services. Therefore, SV's introduction to this case has been primarily to do something about the fact that the information advisory companies today do not indicate who they have as customers, and to what extent, and that this is where we should do something. SV therefore believes that information advisors should be open about who they have as sponsors. Without such transparency, such a lobby register as the Left proposes – and as we in SV think is a good idea – will be necessary, but not sufficient. There should be available customer lists to identify organisations, businesses, public authorities and politicians who have paid for their services. The SV has therefore called for the Government to submit proposals to amend company law with a view to imposing full transparency on companies where all or part of their activities are based on information advice on the part of contracting entities which are in contact with the authorities. Then I register that the parties at Stortinget, with the exception of SV and Left, do not want to go into the proposals to tighten up their professional lobbying against Stortinget. I'm sorry about that. I had especially hoped that the parties that have been in the wind in connection with the fact that former party tops have notified the transition to information consulting companies recently would be involved in highlighting clarity and tightening up their response to this in order to take a clear and clear position on the matter. I'm recording that this isn't happening. However, I have registered with pleasure that there is an agreement in the Presidency to look at the quarantine regulations, and look forward to this review. That means that the matter is moving forward, albeit at a low speed. I therefore take up the minority's proposal from the Presidency.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e332	The relationship between lobbyists and the communication industry on the one hand and politicians on the other has been updated recently by several singles in the media. Not least, the quarantine rules for the members of the Government have been subject to attention. Therefore, the Christian People's Party also believes that it is important and right that the Stortinget today asks the Government to review the experience of the Quarantine Code and present the experience to the Stortinget. With regard to the Left's proposal for a registration scheme for lobbyists, the Christian People's Party has come to the conclusion that the disadvantages with it are more than the benefits. It is an important value that there are low thresholds to address decision-makers. This closeness between people, organisations, businesses and communities on the one hand and central politicians on the other is worth protecting over. The Christian People's Party fears that the registration system proposed can contribute to higher thresholds. The professional actors always manage, but ordinary people can feel that it's wrong to make contact. In addition, a register can help the representatives to limit more precisely who they are listening to, for fear of creating an impression of being ‘in the pocket’ on one or the other. In this case, the registry may inhibit the important and positive part of the lobbying activity. On the other hand, it is a fact that lobbying has evolved to become more professional over the years. Greater openness around this business is desirable, and here the Christian People's Party shares the intentions of the proposers. More transparency can help citizens to better understand important parts of the political decision-making process and to increase confidence in the proper adoption of policy decisions. But the Christian People's Party questions whether the proposed register will be suitable and effective to create the desired transparency. Contact other than the personal encounter at Stortinget shall, for example: not recorded. For example, e-mail and SMS contacts are outside. Meetings held elsewhere also fall outside. Therefore, it is natural to ask whether the scheme outlined will contribute to a proper picture of the lobbying impact on Stortinget. The scheme may also have adverse effects by suspecting that meetings are being held outside the Storting for some reason. Do you do this because you want to avoid registration? It'll be an obvious follow-up from the press. The other side of this is that the meetings and contacts that may be the main reason for public disclosure are deliberately left outside the Storting. None of our Nordic neighbouring countries have introduced such a registration system as proposed. Several countries have taken this debate and have concluded as the majority do today. After these counter-representations, I would like to say that there are also good arguments for the Left's proposal, and the Left shall have the credit for a clear line in the work of transparency in the public domain. The Christian People's Party shares the Left's intention of greater transparency, but is not convinced that the proposed system will be an effective tool in this way. And it's important for the Christian People's Party to keep the threshold low for single citizens when it comes to making contact with decision-makers. In any case, the challenges of lobbyism must be met with clear views of the individual representative and party group with regard to whom you want to meet, etc. If this debate contributes to a greater awareness of this, it is positive and important in itself.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e475	At the request of the Committee on Energy and the Environment, the President will propose that the debate be limited to 70 minutes and that speaking time be allocated as follows: Working Party 25 minutes, Progress Party 15 minutes, Right 10 minutes, Socialist Left Party 5 minutes, Center Party 5 minutes, Christian People's Party 5 minutes and Left 5 minutes. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e486	Norway is to be seen as a major act in marine areas, and we have a great potential to exploit them to produce ocean-based energy either in the form of wind power or other forms of energy. The proposition and the legislative proposal under consideration today include both a strategy for the development of business and energy resources related to ocean-based renewable energy and a proposal for a law on renewable energy at sea, namely the Law on Marine Energy. This is a matter which is primarily characterised by a great deal of cross-political consensus, and I would like to thank the members of the Committee on Energy and the Environment for their good cooperation during the processing process. The case has its background in the climate agreement between the Government Parties and the Right, the Christian People's Party and the Left from January 2008, where the parties agreed to establish a national strategy for power generation from marine mills and other marine renewable energy sources. Such a strategy should address the need for research efforts, consider setting up a dedicated research-driven innovation centre in this area and find a suitable legal instrument, concession system and, not least, define areas that can be used for energy production without over-conflicting with fisheries interests and other industries as well as the marine environment. The legislative proposal is based on Norway’s long experience in the management of hydropower resources, petroleum resources and electricity and gas infrastructure. The proposal involves regulating the entire life cycle of an energy plant, i.e.: both planning, deployment, operation and shutdown, and includes both renewable energy production installations and installations for the conversion and transmission of electrical energy at sea. The law states that the right to use renewable energy resources at sea belongs to the country. The scope of the law is, as a general rule, the sea areas outside the baseline, but it may also be applied within the baseline in some cases. The legislative proposal lays down licensing obligations for the construction, ownership and operation of such facilities. Furthermore, the legislative proposal is based on land assessments and the related impact assessment procedure. When opening the area, actors are invited to apply for a concession. Provision is made for the pre-qualification of tenderers and for an impact assessment. In order to ensure the progress of projects, the authorities will also have the possibility to set deadlines for the start-up of energy installations. Furthermore, the proposal states that the offshore network infrastructure should not be loaded onto shore-based electricity customers. The concession period is proposed to last up to 30 years. On the right, we want to have a concession period of up to 50 years and propose this. The background that we want a longer concession period is two-fold: in the United Kingdom, the concession period is set at 50 years, and we are on the right hand side concerned with providing a level playing field around the North Sea Basin. Furthermore, several hearing bodies have indicated that a longer concession period is preferable in view of the extent and cost of this type of investment in renewable energy in fact and that this requires a long and predictable horizon in relation to the framework conditions. It is positive that work is already under way to assess appropriate areas and that the results of this work will be presented in the last half of 2010, and that a strategic impact assessment of these areas, including a consultation process, will be undertaken in 2011. It is important that this propulsion plan is maintained. In its comments, the Committee has underlined the importance of taking into account the fishing activities in our seas, and I would also like to signal the importance of this from the Chair today. The development of ocean-based renewable energy must take place in good co-existence with other industries, in particular the fishing industry. The proposal has identified the southern part of the North Sea as the area that it expects will initially be most relevant in the use of marine energy, since the area is close to large markets and links to infrastructure are likely to be easier. It is worth noting that a joint committee is asking that the North Sea too be seen with the same intensity, among others. because there may be other wind conditions here that make it interesting in a complete picture of the energy production of the continent. It is good that a law is now in place to exploit marine energy resources outside the baseline. In Europe, in particular in Germany and the United Kingdom, there are large plans for the development of sea wind – not only plans but also financial support schemes that make it attractive to invest. In fact, one expects a market of between 700 and 900 billion DKK to be invested in sea wind energy production in the coming years. This is a market for which Norwegian enterprises and competences are also positioned. Statoil and Statkraft have been granted a concession to further develop and, where appropriate, to expand a large field on Doggerbank in the third round of the British concession, which was recently published. It is good that these companies are paying off, because it is not surprising that the expansion of marine energy in Norway has such a high cost level compared to the possibilities of financial support that it is not realistic to consider a large-scale expansion of marine energy in Norway with the first. It is therefore important to put a demonstration plant for Norwegian technology in place. Finally, I would like to address the proposal from the Right and the Advance Party on a 50-year concession period.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e516	The representative Siri A. Meling has taken up the proposal she referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e519	The climate crisis is facing major challenges for the world’s society. The main challenge is energy. It is a transition from fossil to renewable energy. This is absolutely necessary both for climate reasons and for the prosperity development of the poor part of the world, where the population is growing rapidly. Europe’s choice of energy policies addresses the climate challenges. In addition, Europe has a strong desire to become less dependent on Russia as a dominant gas supplier. Thus, the strongest drivers of European energy policy are climate and energy security. The phased-out coal power must be replaced. Natural gas and renewable energy sources as solar and wind sail as the new energy sources. Wind power has been exploited as a source of energy for decades, but it is only in recent years that expansion has really taken its toll off. Marine wind turbines have become a major and important part of expected future energy production. There are enormous perspectives in the investment in wind power. The rapid expansion creates opportunities for industrial development and employment, which are very fragile at a time when the economy is on the brink after the financial crisis. It is said that in Germany there are now more people employed in wind power generation than in the automotive industry. In England Prime Minister Gordon Brown has said that he expects 70 000 new jobs in this industry. It is expected that by 2020 40 GB of wind will be installed at sea, involving investments of approximately DKK 800 billion. In 20 years' time, the global market for investment in wind power is expected to total NOK 1 700 billion. Europe’s need for more renewable energy provides great opportunities for Norway, both as an energy supplier and as an infrastructure provider. Our traditional investment in hydropower has given us a renewable share in the energy mix that is formidable. In addition, our big gas resources are on the base. Both have a potential for growth. When, in addition to hydropower, we also include our great potential for wind power, we see that Norway's role in European electricity supply can be great. Wind power is precious power compared to hydropower. The cost picture of electricity production and price rental in Norway means that wind power per today and in the near future will play a marginal role in the Norwegian energy balance. This must not lead to hasty conclusions that wind power is not interesting. The massive investment in wind power globally will lead to a product and production development that will reduce costs considerably. There are therefore several reasons why we should commit ourselves politically to the development of wind power, and especially to sea winds. We have a great potential for the production of power at sea. Enova's talking about 1 400 TWh. But in the near future, I think it's most important for us to focus on the industry. We have an industry with offshore experience and power skills that could claim to compete in the job contest. The competence built up through the 40 years of development of the base in the oil and gas industry is worth gold when we are now building on this with renewable energy at sea. But then it is important for our supplier industry, as the rapporteur also pointed out, that the State contributes funds to research and development. The contributions made today include: led to Hywind outside Karmøy, which is the world's first floating sea windmill. The company SWAY has received concession and financial support for the development of a huge windmill in Eyegarden, where it is testing up-sized windmills, which is absolutely necessary to bring down the costs of windmills in the long term. We have research centres for environmentally friendly energy. We have various networks of industry and research around Norway working on this, which is large and attracts an incredible number of businesses and business environments, as well as research and development environments, which are involved and driving development, and which are involved in and drive a transformation in industry to address the challenges facing the supplier side of wind power. The role of Statoil and Statkraft in the development of sea-based wind power in the United Kingdom is well known. We are in many ways completely technological and when this is to be expanded, but it is a tough competition for our industry to get into this sector. I think there might be reason to mention that the experience we have in operating and maintaining installations in the North Sea today, will also be gold worth when these windmills come up and will be operated in the future. Then, from the industrial and research environments, the need for test and demonstration facilities has been highlighted on a larger scale than what we have today. I think it's important to get there. I think that, for example, The marine sul project, on which the Western Alliance has been granted a concession and is ready to expand in the North West, could be a good place to set up a test and demo plant. We have also received input from the industry, such as this “Demo 2020” note – a test and demonstration facility with eight turbines worth DKK 2.5 to 4 billion. These are interesting insights, and I think it is important that we, on the government side, also participate, and that we come in cash, quickly, with support for this product development so that Norwegian industry can take the role that we hope they will have when development and expansion begins, especially in the British sector. The second role we politicians play is the role of regulators. The legislative proposal that we are dealing with here is, of course, the most important part of it, and it goes into the series of traditions we have for the management of the country's natural resources, especially on the energy side. We make it very clear that these resources belong to the state. The framework provides for democratic control over its development and over the management of resources. A balanced use of land is envisaged, taking into account both the environment, other industries and the conditions that may be involved. There is a broad political consensus on the law, as it stands. The only question I want to comment on here now is the question of concession time. This issue has been addressed in the proposition and has been discussed there, and it is clear that there are good arguments for both 30 years and 50 years. What is important to carry with it is that it is added that the normal concession should be extended when applied for, if there are no special conditions that warrant otherwise. Finally, I would like to thank the rapporteur for a good and tidy job in the Committee during the examination of this matter. I think it's been a teaching process for all of us, and it's a good bill that we're now dealing with.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e552	I would like to start by thanking the case rapporteur and the Committee for a good cooperation on an important and forward-looking law that the Law on Marine Energy really is. The Progress Party agrees with the Government that it is important to establish a law regulating the use of land and the different forms of energy at sea. The Maritime Renewable Energy Production Bill shall provide the State with the legal framework to grant concessions and otherwise regulate conditions relating to the planning, deployment, operation and decommissioning of renewable energy installations. The legislative proposal also deals with the transformation and transmission of electricity from the place of production and further towards the consumer. The legislative proposal is largely based on land assessments and related impact assessment procedures. The Government writes in its proposition that it wants Norway to develop further as an environmentally friendly and climate-friendly nation and to be the leader in the development of environmentally friendly energy. The EU is a driver of renewable energy with its 2020 objectives. By 2020, EU countries will produce 20 pst. more renewable energy than today. Very much of this renewable energy will come from wind turbines, and especially wind turbines at sea. As the government writes, it will probably take a long time for us in Norway to build wind turbines on a large scale at sea, and especially liquid wind turbines, which are both costly and technically difficult. The electricity price from wind turbines at sea will be too high for Norway. Nevertheless, it is of great importance that the Norwegian supplier industry prepares to supply equipment, among other things. sea windmills. The incorporation of Statoil and Statkraft into the UK giant windmill project at Doggerbank could have a significant impact on the Norwegian supplier industry. A large part of the global climate challenges will be addressed with renewable energy. This is also agreed by the Progress Party. Greater renewable energy production will help reduce global climate emissions. In the long term, sea wind will be able to provide a significant contribution to the renewable energy produced today. The Progress Party will particularly point to Norway as a major power of energy export. Hydropower has been and is the pillar of domestic energy consumption, both for industry and for households. Norway has a high potential from both regulated and non-regulatory energy sources. Reserves in hydropower are not insignificant and should be expanded in the opinion of the Progress Party. Protected watercourses need to be re-assessed in the light of today's more environmentally friendly development opportunities. Hydropower energy is cheap to produce, and should be expanded to a much greater extent than at present. Hydropower will become even more important when the EU renewable directive is put into effect. In windless days and when the need for electricity is particularly high within the EU, a large Norwegian surplus of hydropower can be the best guarantee for lower greenhouse gas emissions from EU countries. The alternative for Norwegian hydropower to replace EU wind power would have to be coal power to a large extent. Wind power from land-based installations also has a very high energy potential and will be prioritised before installations are built on deep water in Norwegian waters. The Progress Party believes there is a great need for a thorough review of remaining water and wind resources in Norway. When such surveys are available, priority will be facilitated. Without such priorities, it would be difficult to plan and develop a satisfactory electricity transmission network for the consumer. The view of the Progress Party is that the use of hydropower is most environmentally friendly and cheapest. Therefore, this type of renewable energy must come first. This view is shared by many in the industry and among leading economists. In a March issue of Technical Weekly, BI professor Torger Reve says, among other things. Norway should invest in the environmental technology where we are best. He goes on to say that hydropower is the best renewable energy we have, and that there is no kind of renewable energy production we can do better in this country. It is important that operators in the Norwegian sector are given the same conditions as in other neighbouring countries. Therefore, the Progress Party, together with the Right, proposes that the concession period be set at 50 years from the start of the facilities, against the 30 years of the Government. So in both of these proposals, there is an opportunity to apply for an extension of the concession period. The United Kingdom, with which it's natural to compare, has a 50-year concession period. The Advance Party, together with the Right and Christian People's Party, believes that the Petroleum Authority will be responsible for the health, environment and safety of marine energy installations. Summing up, the Progress Party supports the Government's proposal for the Marine Energy Act. It is important that the legislation is in front of the need and is in line with the legislation of other countries in this area. Standards for power generation, not least for a network for the collection and supply of wind power, must be developed together with EU countries. In the bill, the government makes a reasonable progress where the government itself is to assess the applicant companies, and ultimately to grant concessions. The party of progress shares here the government's vision.

The fact that the state's resources benefit the community is not a general rule on a global scale. In a number of countries, we still see the results of a wild policy, where a few earn large sums of money to reap values that should have belonged to many more. Maybe it has extra big symbolic power in the case of the oil. Nearly no country can refer to the distribution of oil wealth that Norway can. On the contrary, oil in many parts of the world is associated with unrest, social instability and conflict — even war. Oil is an example of the fact that resource wealth itself does not necessarily create wealth for people – it is political choices that do. Therefore, it is quite natural that Norway should ensure the rights of use of our offshore wind resources. It is part of a proud tradition, where overall planning and politics create values for the good of the community. It is therefore quite natural that the law should be based on elements of petroleum legislation. It is good for professional environments and businesses, because the links are many between today's offshore activities, our management of hydropower and the future energy production at sea. Cooperation between Statkraft and Statoil in the Doggerbank project illustrates this. It is also good for democracy, because it does not become international capital forces alone that dictate the conditions for its use of resources. It is also important to ensure comprehensive planning of offshore energy production. Germany has a law that gives public governance with the development of networks and infrastructure, England does not. For each actor, marginalisations that match their own needs are, of course, the most profitable. Overall planning is not their responsibility. On the other hand, it is in the interests of society that the development of infrastructure at sea, the potential supergrid of the future and the distribution of power at sea are a public responsibility. We in SV therefore believe that this perspective is important, and that it is natural to look at, for example, in the extension of the work done today. State network as system operator. This issue is one of several to be addressed now, together with the relevant areas of renewable energy production at sea, environmental, food and fisheries interests, etc. An inter-ministerial working group – it sounds very dry, but I think the work is exciting – will present a report on this already in the autumn, and it is only hope that we will be able to keep the pressure ahead. Starting a process that turns into a new energy standard for Norway requires that we keep our commitment and keep the grip on the matter, beyond the debate today. It requires financial arrangements that take into account both long-term profitability and the high initial costs. This requires that we maintain a focus on the knowledge environments in Norway and the enterprises that are spearheads, among other things. SWAY in Bergen and Smartmotor in my hometown. It requires testing and demonstration facilities and the active involvement of industry, which have a natural role in the development of this energy. We know that the theoretical potential for renewable marine energy in Norway is enormous. I think we have a pretty long time. But it is up to us to ensure that this potential is exploited in a socially beneficial way, in a way that creates new Norwegian jobs, new challenges for our technology environments and for the supplier industry. The law we adopt today is a first step on the road, but above all it is a commitment that we should take on with the greatest zeal and joy.

In addition to the technological challenges, the environment, safety and industrial activities shall also be taken into account. The Act states that energy installations are to be planned, built and disposed of in balance with the environment, safety, fisheries and maritime transport. The production of marine energy is at an early stage, and we still have knowledge needs. We don't know enough about the consequences of offshore wind power for seabirds. Southern areas of the North Sea will have the shortest transfer distances to the consumption markets in Europe, but have challenges related to co-variability with other major planned sea-area expansions. North Sea areas have longer transfer distances to markets, but due to other wind and weather systems, they will have less covariance problems in electricity exports and also have larger wind resources. In both sea areas more knowledge is also needed on possible electrification of the cap with offshore wind power. Therefore, it is important that future land studies assess possible areas in the North Sea in addition to the southern North Sea, in the light of the technical and economic conditions indicated in the proposal, and in both sea areas, assessments are made of the possible electrification of the sea-based wind base. Today, we have taken an important step on the way to establishing the basis for a future large scale Norwegian wind power generation. The legal framework is in place. We have learned from our long experience in the management of hydropower resources, petroleum resources and electricity and gas infrastructure. The law on renewable offshore energy production is based on this. Natural resources shall remain in public ownership and shall be managed for the benefit of the Community. We have taken an important step towards green jobs, green energy production and green environmental technologies.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e671	In June last year, the government presented the proposal on the Law on Renewable Energy Production at Sea – the Law on Marine Energy. This document consists of a strategic part that points out the direction of offshore wind in Norway and a bill that provides a basis for the concession treatment of off-line installations. It is a historic event that we are today completing the law for the disposal of our natural resources for energy purposes for the benefit of the community. First came the hydroelectric power with groundbreaking legislation 100 years ago, then the petroleum legislation early in the 1970s, and now we are completing to secure the rights of exploitation of our wind resources at sea. At the same time, the law indicates that we are now investing in renewable energy into the ocean! 50 years in advance, it is quite possible that looking back in history, you can see that March 11, 2010, is a date that is clearly remembered in that context. There are good reasons why Norway should invest in offshore wind power. The energy potential in Norwegian sea areas is very high. Our business life is based on a technology that gives us a good starting point for actively participating in the development of renewable energy production at sea. The legislative proposal provides for public control and control of the disposal of renewable energy resources at sea and is based on elements of the petroleum legislation. The State shall provide an early assessment of other relevant societal and environmental interests before opening up areas for applications. It will reduce potential conflicts, ensure coordination with network development and increase predictability for actors. The law is a framework, and additional requirements are necessary, including: as regards health, environment and safety. In addition, Enova has set up a demonstration programme for offshore power generation with financial support, including: to the demonstration projects Hywind and SWAY. Last September, the NVE was assigned to lead a directorate group with the Directorate for Nature Management, the Directorate for Fisheries, the Coastal Agency and the Oil Directorate. This group shall rough-sort the areas concerned for renewable production at sea. It shall include, inter alia: take into account other land use interests, the environment, sea depth, wind resources, market potential and the relationship with the transmission system, etc. As the Committee points out, it is important that a broad assessment be made of the various sea areas in the Norwegian economic zone. The group shall present its work this autumn. Marine wind land assessments and offshore transmission system planning are closely linked. The main principle proposed in this round is that on-shore network customers should not cover the costs of a transmission system at sea. It is very positive that a joint committee goes in to assess the area of the northern North Sea as well in the first round. Large areas are needed for the development of offshore wind power. Different wind systems and depths make different solutions necessary. Southern areas of the North Sea have the shortest transmission distances to the consumption market in Europe, but at the same time they have challenges related to co-variability with other major planned seas. Area in the northern North Sea has a longer transfer distance to the market, but due to other wind and weather systems, there will be less covariance problems in power exports, and one also has greater wind power resources in the northern part of the North Sea. This flexibility is important from the start. The potential for offshore wind power, both from fixed and floating installations, is much greater than the domestic demand for electricity in the coming years. Against the background of Europe’s high renewable energy needs, this will provide great opportunities for exports of electricity from offshore wind power generation in the European electricity market. Norwegian offshore wind power can play an important role in meeting the European renewable energy demand in the future, both to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and to ensure Europe’s security of electricity supply. Good electricity transmission infrastructure is crucial for the development and development of renewable energy production at sea, security of supply and the rational use of resources. A new 3rd generation core network for the transmission of power in sea cables along the coast, a so-called supernet, will be important to achieve this. It is very positive that Norway joined the planning of a European super-network in the North Sea in early February. This means that Norwegian renewable energy can have an important place in the European energy balance. Norwegian hydropower is pointed to as an important factor in the European power system, and Norway needs to facilitate the exploitation of this potential. This means that we should already start planning a Norwegian supernet further north along the Norwegian coast. It will add both Norwegian hydropower and Norwegian wind power to the European super network, and it can help get more participants in the split layer to generate grid capacity further north. It will also make it easier to assess further the development of wind power, and it may reduce the need for the largest lines within the country. It'll be positive in many ways. We see today that it is too expensive and too bad technology to exploit wind energy in an economically defenceable way. Therefore, the Right would like to facilitate demonstration facilities both to test out technology and to pre-qualify Norwegian operators for markets outside Norway. Norwegian companies show great willingness to participate in technological developments before sea wind power, and Demo 2020 is a good example of this. They represent more than a hundred industrial companies, five universities and four research communities, from Verdal in the north to Kristiansand in the south. They have established unique cooperation and wish to establish a Norwegian test and demonstration programme for marine wind and ocean energy. As the Council of State notes, the technology environment in Måløy in Sogn and Fjordane are also directed towards offshore wind. The sister companies Easy Form and Best Mould design and produce original noise pedals for windmills for customers abroad. Stadt Towing Tank has an 182 meter long test pool and launches newly developed technology, among other things. into offshore wind. Brothers AS developed the new concept Sea Bridge for the entrapment of offshore wind turbines from the boat, while Måløy Verft builds service and supply ships for wind farms. Shipowner Fredrik Odfjell in FOB Shipping has booked the first boat and has option on three more. Odfjell says to the newspaper Firda that the industry believes that within three to four years there is a need for 100 new boats for wind farms. It shows some of the potential of these industrial developments, and it is also an important part of a future investment in marine wind energy.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e835	Today, we are dealing with a legislative and regulatory framework for future renewable energy production in our marine areas. We have traditions in Norway regarding this type of legislation. It started with the concession laws early in the 1900s for the use of hydropower. It continued with the law relating to the harvesting of petroleum deposits in the 1970s, and it continues to deal with this proposition today. The conflict between private capitalist interests and the interests of the community becomes apparent in such cases. We all remember how anti-interest attitudes have flared up in the treatment of the home-fall institute in recent years. When it comes to billions of values, it's more important than ever to be ideologically conscious and keep an eye on the community's best interests. When it blows at the worst and the pressure is greatest, a strong back and a clear political anchoring are required. For the Workers' Party, this is the result of an ideological anchoring and conscious choice when we are clear that the country's large and natural energy resources are our property, and are therefore to benefit the community as much as possible. In our opinion, it is fundamentally unfair whether such large values should be in the pockets of individuals or companies. In order to avoid precisely this happening, clear legislation is needed, both in relation to any ownership rights and the framework and driving rules, when it comes to who will be responsible for the specific harvesting of resources. In addition, it is essential to have a tax regime that ensures a fair distribution of the profits and values harvested. In addition to the perspectives I have now mentioned, a good framework is important in terms of attracting private capital and private stakeholders. Stability and predictability may be the main factor in successful Public Private Cooperation. It is difficult today to have a secure and definite view of the future potential of renewable offshore energy production, whether offshore wind turbines or other renewable energy sources in the ocean. Here the prognosis cracks enormously, from the greatest optimism to the most negative pessimist. Regardless of who should be given the right, it is important to have an industrial thinking around what can come from renewable energy sources from the marine areas in the future. Norway has a high level of industrial competence in offshore installations and solutions. In addition to this, we are a great energy nation, with all the advantages it brings. We must manage that with all the responsibility in it. We need to ensure that this competence makes us world leaders in renewable energy. This gives us great industrial opportunities that we must not let go of. We can create thousands of jobs and get great value creation in this area in the future. We have to take care of this opportunity, and the treatment of today's case is an important part of this work. The framework for the production of renewable energy at sea, which we are adopting today, I think will prove to be very important for our future value creation, so that we can have great values for distribution in the future as well.

It is a proposal that has been investigated in the past. Prior to the 2004 reform, the transfer of municipal tasks to the municipal sector was also assessed. The competence and capacity of the municipality was one of the arguments against such a transfer. Small municipalities are initially difficult to obtain qualified staff, and in addition it was not realistic to imagine that all municipalities should have their own institutional services. It was initially difficult for the county councils, and there was no reason to believe that it would be easier for the municipalities. For the largest municipalities, which have a population base equal to or greater than many counties, the Ministry at the hearing considered that taking over the tasks of county councils in the field of child protection and possibly the area of family protection would be feasible. But with the then municipal structure, the characteristic of a large number of small municipalities, there was also reason to ask questions about the competence and capacity of the small municipalities to take over the county responsibility. The community perspective becomes important in the evaluation. We have one child protection in Norway today, and this evaluation must also reflect this. The results of the evaluation will be available in 2011. It will provide us with a better basis for assessing the issues on which the representatives of the Left are also concerned. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e1454	The state of Norway is established in the territory of two peoples, Samer and Nordmann. Both peoples have the same right and the same requirement to develop their culture and language. Samemen, in addition, are indigenous peoples who have a call for special cultural protection under international law. The merger is therefore also established by the law of partnership in order to comply with the Constitution § 110a. This gives the whole thing a special status as a popularly elected body with great freedom. I would like to take this opportunity to also thank the Committee for good cooperation, and I would like to express my satisfaction that there is such a broad consensus in this country in terms of Sami issues. An overall committee, i.e. all lots, with the exception of the Party of Advancement, shall agree on the observations in this case. The 2008 Joint Annual Report sets out the work of the Sameting, the tasks of the Sameting and economic and administrative challenges. The communication covers a wide range of areas, ranging from work on discrimination and gender equality, cross-border co-operation, regional policy issues, land and environment policy issues, through culture, cultural memory protection and languages, to kindergartens, schools and the preservation of traditional knowledge. It is not possible, within the framework of this article, to address anything affecting the Sami communities. I have to draw up something, and it is not that what is not mentioned here is not considered important. The majority of the Committee has taken note of the need to clarify the budgetary procedures applicable between the Government and the Sameting. I understand that the Sameting, like the municipalities, needs a certain predictability in the budget framework from year to year. The same applies to the need to clarify the framework for next year's activities so early that it is possible to make sound budgeting for next year. These needs can be understood, regardless of the formal position of the Sameting. There has been a working group in turn that has looked at different models that can be used, but no agreement has been reached between the Sameting and the Government in the consultations on the issue. Hopefully, a clarification of budgetary procedures will come soon, and it is hoped that this will meet the needs of greater predictability and some earlier clarifications for the budget work of the Alliance. Knowledge of the Sami community, and in particular knowledge of special Sami conditions, is an important decision-making base for us as politicians. It is therefore important to continue the work on Sami statistics. I am pleased that the Ministry of Renewal, Administration and Church will contribute to ensuring further funding of this work. The majority of the Committee has also noted that the long-term objective of the Sameting is that the production of Sami related statistics becomes a firm and established activity. The proposal for new mineral law aroused great debate both in Finnmark and in Sami environments. It was also expressed in demanding consultations between the Government and the Sameting. The plenary session also ended with not being able to support the bill. However, that said, there is reason to respect the considerations of the Sameting on the need for long-term local ring-effects and participation to ensure the preservation of Sami interests. The majority of the Committee has also indicated that this will be an important prerequisite for mineral activities. Finally, I would like to mention the importance of following up the Government's action plan on Sami languages. The plan was presented in May 2009 and has a working time of five years. Language is one of the factors — if not the most important — ensuring a culture. The Sami languages, especially lukewarm and southern Sami, are threatened, in the sense that they are spoken only by hundreds of people. The measures described in the action plan must be followed up and will be followed closely by me. With this I have, on behalf of the majority of the committee, the pleasure of recommending the vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e1682	==References== 34,000 farms are empty in Norway today, while surveys show that about 400,000 people could be interested in taking over these and, not least, using them. As former municipal and county politicians – even though some years ago – I was often involved in these kinds of things, where there was a seller and a buyer; a seller who, for one or more reasons, wanted to sell his property to someone who would like to live and further develop this property. They agreed on the price, the acquisition date, what should be included in the deal, that the seller would possibly have to live there for a while, or get other rights for a certain period – i.e. that everything was settled between the seller and the purchaser. Often the papers were written by e.g. a lawyer, so that everything was orderly and thoughtful. Then the case came to the municipality for political treatment with a recommendation from the councillor and often with a refusal, or a partial refusal, because for example. the price was too high in relation to the return on the farm or that some of the property would be sold as additional land to one or more neighbours. This often led to a political majority in favour of the position of the councillor, and it resulted in that there was no trade, because the seller did not want to sell the farm at an too low amount. After all, this was a work of life that sometimes several generations had built up. It could also be that the trade did not happen because the buyer wanted to buy the entire farm and not only part of it, or it could be too short for the buyer to move to the property. So it often ended up with the seller, i.e. owner, kept the farm, leased away the land and had logging in the forest when it was desirable. But what's bad is that the building mass, both housing and operating buildings, was only used occasionally, and the decay began. The same can be said about the cultural landscape both in and around the tuna, spreading to the entire property. Thus begins the evaporation of our districts. No new takeover. The years go by, and by the time of death some years later, the maturity has come too far. No one wants to take over a drop-out property, and the farm is sold as extra land to the neighbor. In the Rendalen municipality of Hedmark County, the average age is 48 years, while in Jären is 35 years. This says something about if you are to have growth, a rule and law must follow along in time. If you want to work not only for a few farmers, but also for electricians, carpenters, people in shops, health professionals, people in good associations and sports, we have to say yes, that is, have a yes mentality, even if some of those who take over a farm don't live there all the time or not just run with what the farm has produced all the years. Many people probably read in the Bondebladet a while ago about him who got NOK 3000 in a fine every day because he was running with lofting inside the barn. As I understood it, this was a good enough way of life for him, but the authorities said no. Unfortunately, he is not the only one who gets no, when you want to expand your income. Another example is from my municipality. A small user wanted to lay out a small part of the property to seven cottages. He had potential buyers, and he got permission from the municipality, but only to five plots. Then there was nothing of it, for his income by selling these plots was in empty number. 6 and empty number 7th@item: inlistbox The other covered the costs of reprocessing and selling costs. I can frame up many situations where people want to buy empty farms, but they want to some extent to use them in a slightly different way than before. It would create the growth that the districts need. It would create quality of life, and not least it would create light in the windows. It would also create optimism in those who already live there. It's often the case that if the neighbor picks up, builds something new, infects it. Maybe you don't do that much, but something you want to do. This would again have created an increase in value that both the owner and his descendants would have benefited from, and not least society in general. It would also do a lot for the tourists, and what we want to show them. No one would be proud to show up an area or a building where many of the 34 000 empty farms are standing. In addition to the 34 000 empty farms, we have a sad development in the number of farms being run. In the last four years, while the Central Party has had the Minister of Agriculture and Food, and we have had the same government as now, there have been 4,419 fewer farms. I think it has a clear connection with the empty farms. When it does not pay to clean up buildings and properties because, among other things, it does. are maximum selling prices, give up, or at best, keep them at the same level. Some opponents – and perhaps the vast majority – claim that with the policies of the Progress Party, many of these farms would be bought up by rich people and used only for resorts. Yes, it may be true that some of them would have been, but there would have been light in the windows in parts of the year, the buildings would have been taken care of, cleaned up and expanded. It would create work and positive ring-effects, and the earth would be led away to the neighbor, so that he had his use strengthened – a win-win situation for everyone. The services offered in the village would have flourished, and that would have served everyone. It has been said by current and former governments that agriculture should be the backbone of the rural settlement. That was perhaps the case before, but that spine is today very fragile and little tolerable. I think the dawning peasant revolt we see today, not only is political trouble in front of an agricultural settlement, but something more serious. And I mean the law we have today, it keeps this balance in a good way. At the same time, I would like to stress that it is a continuous process to ensure that the law is adapted to the challenges of our time. Last year, the Stortinget adopted major legislative changes, including: in such a way that fewer properties were subject to the obligation of concession, dwelling and immovable property. In addition, housing rules were simplified so that they have become better tools for municipalities in order to ensure housing on farm. The amendments were adopted with the support of a large majority in the Grand Duchy. In addition to this, I sent for approx. a month ago, a new circular containing changes in price control under the concession law. I have decided that price control should be avoided if the purchase price for built-up agricultural property is less than NOK 1500000. Thus, the lower limit of when price controls are to be carried out has increased to twice that. This means that smaller properties, where the housing function is more prominent than the economic aspect, can be priced more precisely on the market. This allows several small properties to be sold. It is thus based on the idea that some properties can be offered in a larger market where the desire for a lifestyle property can also be cared for – while other properties with a greater resource base are reserved for those who want to have their profession – and activity – in agriculture. These changes are not based on light-winded solutions where community concerns are absent. However, in my opinion, together with a deliberate use of the rules in the municipalities and active local community brokerage, they can contribute to the resettlement of empty farms. I trust that the municipalities are aware of this, and that it must be worked purposely over time to achieve such results.
