ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e2002	In the last decades, the number of reindeer in Finnmark has increased significantly, and this is considered to be a problem, as a desire for sustainable resource use, i.e. a cut-off number that is best suited to bets. It has been such that the individual reindeer determines for themselves how many puree to be slaughtered. This was and is not a problem as long as there is a surplus of pasturage, but when the total reindeer becomes too large in relation to grazing access, there is a problem with an increased risk of loss of animals. There are several reasons why a practitioner would like to have more clean. One of the obvious is the desire for better income in subsequent years. Another reason is the need to insure against the losses that you know years will come about. As your own insurance, the solution is to have so many clean ones that you can withstand the loss without losing the basis of existence. Each of these causes is logical and reasonable in itself, and as an individual, most people in the same situation will try to increase their own herd of reindeer. The problem arises when everyone thinks the same and everyone increases their pet numbers. This in itself will increase the risk and accelerate losses due to reduced nutritional availability. So when everyone increases, one will sooner or later achieve the opposite effect of what you want. Instead of insuring yourself against loss, accelerating and increasing a loss of pure. The problems of adapting to a limited natural basis are not a specific phenomenon in reindeer management. One finds clear parallels in other parts of society. In Norway, it is most obvious to compare overfishing with herring and cod. Although the individual fisherman realized that the catches were too large, and that it was wrong, the individual fisherman was powerless. If a fisherman stops his fish, the other fisherman fishes the last cod. The logical strategy is to participate to ensure that he does not become a double loser: the stock is overfished without even getting part of what is being harvested. In principle, there are two possible solutions to the problem of adapting activities to the resource base. One solution is that the nutritionists themselves agree on a highest degree of purity that they distribute between themselves. They must then introduce the necessary control measures internally. This requires a transparent grouping of nutritionists. This is useful, for example: not to bet on such a solution for cod fishing in the Barents Sea. The second possible solution to the resource adjustment is that an outside authority determines allowances allocating the limited resources. At the same time, they must implement the necessary control measures to ensure compliance with the rules. Professor Reidar Elven expresses the following to the research magazine Apollon: ‘Samian reindeer management damages the biodiversity of Finnmark’s territory. The problem is the size of the reindeer flocks. The winter pastures throughout the interior of Finnland are almost destroyed. [[...]] Most of the reinlavene is already eaten or drained by reindeer. [[...]] The most hard hit areas are already desert. [[...]] The degradation of Finnmark is therefore an ecological disaster. [[...]] [[...]] It may take up to fifty years before the reinlavene is back The operation has already been unbalanced with nature for at least 30 to 40 years.» Rolf Anker Ims, project manager for Ecosystem Finnmark, who combines the fields of ecology and social sciences to find the reasons for and consequences of reindeer variations within both disciplines, states: ‘The project has collected data from slaughterhouses, inter alia, to assess whether plurality in reindeer flocks leads to lower slaughter weights on the animals. Such a relationship has been widely accepted in the past and was also clearly demonstrated in the Finnish Ecosystem. However, the study has also shown some of the biological mechanisms behind the low slaughter weights. Foetals of sims slaughtered in the winter were examined, and the foetuses were found to be smaller in sims from high-density populations than in the low-density populations. So pasturage has effects on the reindeer already in Mother's life. – In addition, we see that the teeth of pure from high density areas in Finnmark wear faster than in all other comparable cervid populations. In fact, the teeth wear down to the gums in just a few years. – The reason is the grazing, which means that the cleaners have to eat more high-fibrous diet. The animals are also likely to get some sand and stone as a result of degraded pasture vegetation. “The poor teeth make the animals even less able to make use of their poor diet Some researchers, for example, believe that very high grazing pressure over time could tip the ecosystem in a positive direction by gradually being dominated by productive grazing plants such as nutritious grass species. [[...]] – We have refuted this theory. Our project clearly shows that for many pure people, grazing is reduced, even within the most productive summer pastures The social part of the project has also killed some myths. [[...]] Among other things, the theory that participation, voluntary agreements and economic incentives, so-called ‘gulrot instruments’, are more effective than the good old whip. – The cleaning industry itself actively participates in the process of setting framework conditions for the industry, and at the same time expresses dissatisfaction with the renumbering adjustments. – It turns out that there are many disagreements about how to determine the purity, which parts of the industry should reduce the number and which instruments should be used, says IMS. He also believes that the co-management system has led to greater power for people with many clean powers, while the subsidy and compensation system favours large flocks. This is particularly good after so-called disaster winters. After such winters, people who are both large will be left with the most animals for further production and will receive the bulk of the compensation from the authorities. – In reality, the system operates in such a way that those who operate least sustainably are favoured by the State. The authorities' intention has probably been to help the reindeer industry in a sustainable way, but the authorities have not been able to see the long-term consequences of the management of Rein driften as an important cultural carrier in Finnmark. [[...]] At the same time, the industry must meet production requirements that coincide with the rest of society. “The clean-up industry is at the intersection of new and old times, and is therefore facing many challenges, not least in the context of climate change. A warmer climate in Finnmark can have a great effect on the winter pastures for reindeer. [[...]] – Mildness with subsequent frost or snow on a broad scale are challenges that will require much of both industry and government Ecologist Rolf Anker Ims does not conceal that the project Ecosystem Finnmark has been challenging. This is the professional community that has joined the system. The reindeer document more infested game damage to their animals than before. From last year to this year, the compensation has increased by 40 pst. The claims for compensation for the last reindeer year have now been dealt with. This year, about 20,000 pure people killed by predatory game were compensated, and a total of 57 million were given. DKK in compensation for lost animals, compared with 35 million. Last year – a big increase. The reindeer’s last year of reindeer operation has been able to document the damage caused by predatory game of 1 300 pure, and this is an increase of approx. 50 pst. from last year. Of these, about half are documented globular lesions. An estimated 80,000 animals have been lost for various reasons during the last year of reindeer management. Of these, the reindeers have applied for compensation for 65 000 animals. That is, there are 213 animals dying every day in Finnmark. If this is not enough, Norwegian taxpayers must also pay carnivores in the hundreds of thousands of krona class to Swedish reindeers. I asked my colleagues in the Nordic Council if they would pay compensation for Norwegian Sami on Swedish soil. The answer was that they were not naive, nor did they have access to Norwegian oil billions. Where is this going to end? We need to put in place effective and fair control mechanisms. Such a proposal should be to earmark reindeer electronically in the same way as sheep. Every reindeer has to get a separate number. Only in that way can we verify the number. We have to start a large-scale slaughter of tamrein, especially in the west, so that the number of tamrein can come down to such a level that we can have a sustainable nourishment. Instead of sustainably developing the reindeer industry in Finland, Stortinget and today’s government choose not to do anything. The government should give priority to caring for the grazing and resource base, but today’s government chooses a policy that does not respect sustainability. On the contrary, one chooses to deal with Sami rights and requirements and ignores the principle of sustainability. This is a bear service to the reindeer industry in general, but is also devastating for all other food establishments. The situation in Finnmark is such that an industry that receives more than 100 million annually. kr in State subsidies, has been granted exclusive rights, while operating exclusively on all other developments and the establishment of businesses that have the right to life. When will the Government start taking responsibility for its failed policy?
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e2108	First of all, I would like to thank the representative for the interpellation, which gives us an opportunity to discuss the premises for sustainable reindeer management. Initially, I would also like to point out that Finnmark is the country's largest reindeer farm, and that reindeer farming here is a significant economic and employment factor. Regardless of the reindeer discussion, it will in any case be necessary to deal in Finnmark in different ways with reindeer management as an important part of society. The purity of parts of Finnmark has been on the agenda for several decades. In St.meld. No 13 for 1974–1975, About an action plan for the Sámi settlement areas, the situation of reindeer management is described in more detail. I'm not going to go into everything that was expressed there, but to illustrate, I'm going to reproduce the following passus: «At the same time, the adverse effects of over-taxation of the pasturage are reinforced by the downwards of the slaughter weight of the animals and, in order to maintain the economic yield, the stock is increased. This vicious circle can only be broken if the number of units of use in the reindeer operation is brought down slightly, while at the same time there is a reasonable restriction on the size of the flock. Such regulations must be applied for under the law on reindeer management.’;» The questions of purity have later been raised and discussed in different contexts, not least from the Stockholm Chair. A number of different measures have been put in place over the years, including: the five-year migration programme for Inner Finnmark, which was a follow-up to St.meld. No 28 for 1991-1992 with the title: Sustainable reindeer management. As the representative of Tåldal points out, the Riksrevision has also had a comprehensive process in relation to the issues related to the resource situation in these areas. The representative, Tjældal, asks questions about what I as the Council of State would do in the matter. First, I have to make it clear that it was the Rein driftsgestiget which decided in 2002 on the maximum reindeer number for the reindeer grazing districts in the West-Finland reindeer grazing area, with a total of 64,300 animals. For eastern Finland, the corresponding figure is 74,200 animals, but the figures for the districts here are fixed at different times. I also consider it important to clarify – and it is important – that it is only in some areas of Finnmark that the reindeer number is too high in relation to the grazing basis. It is therefore important to be nuanced when referring to the reindeer industry. Clearly, in most districts and areas there are no problems related to the reindeer number. As I mentioned in the introduction, the issue has been linked to too high a reindeer in parts of Finnmark on the agenda for many decades. Many governments and government councils have been working on these issues for many years without solving the matter. This shows, in my view, that, first of all, one faces a very complex problem, and, second, that it is necessary to think completely new and take a completely different approach from what has been done until quite recently. We must be able to say clearly that such a distinction has been made under the new reindeer law that we have now received. In my opinion, the possibility of taking a completely different approach to sewage treatment policy was clearly demonstrated by the adoption by Stortinget of a new reindeer management law in June 2007. Shortly after the government took over the responsibility of Stoltenberg I, an initiative was taken to adopt a new reindeer management law. Such an initiative was received positively from several teams because it had been discovered over many years that the tool that had been used until then had not been sufficiently developed. Not least, the industry itself was in favour of the follow-up of the government. The new reindeer management law came into force on 1 July 2007. A significant change from the previous reindeer management law is that a completely new scheme for reindeer fixing has been introduced in the new law. The rules of use shall determine the figures for each side according to the area of the site. The clean figure thus gets a direct anchoring in the available surface at side level. This figure shall then be approved by the Rein driftssteuurt. Prior to work on rules of use and renumbering, criteria were developed for determining reindeer numbers in cooperation with industry. These criteria shall be applied at all levels of the decision-making process. In this context, I just have to point out that the cooperation between authorities and industry during the establishment of criteria is both new and unique. The new reindeer management law is responsible and involves the reindeer industry to a much greater extent than before. Experience from previous processes has just shown that the lack of cooperation and the lack of involvement of industry has been a clear and contributing cause for a lack of achievement. I am convinced that it is important to avoid a top-down attitude when working on this kind of thing, if a person wants real results and not conflict. Work is currently in progress on the preparation and authorisation of rules of use. The districts had a deadline of 1 July 2009 to submit the rules of use. What number you're going to get out with here remains to be seen. However, in my view, the process it has set up provides the best possible basis for a responsible pasturage that is sustainable over time. Once this process has been completed, a reduction in purity is assumed where this is too high in relation to the established one. It must also be mentioned that the reindeer management law has its own procedures for reindeer reduction, procedures that are far more effective than under old law. Penalties for those not complying with public decisions are also much more nuanced and effective than under old law. This gives the authorities a much better tool than you've had up to now. I will follow developments in the future carefully and continuously assess progress and effectiveness. In extending this, I would also like to point out the need to create a calm and a good environment for interaction in this important work. If the instruments of the new law produce the desired results, it will take some time to learn in all details. But at least you don't have to get into a situation where you give up before you try out the new tool that the law gives. In my opinion, the procedure we are now following is the right one, and that one should also work with in the future. Nor can I see that some other actors have presented an alternative and credible arrangement that would indicate that we should now change course. In addition, work is under way on a major communication on agricultural policy to be presented in 2011. In this message, reindeer operations will also have their natural place. The Communication will carry out a broad and thorough review of the reindeer management policy to see if it is necessary to adjust the price or take new action. In this work, it will be important for many good premises suppliers. In addition to recordings from the reindeer myself, I would like, among other things. point out that work is in progress in the light of research institutions that it would be natural to familiarise themselves with and involve themselves in the assessments. When this message is available, I hope we have a good debate in the Grand Duchy on key issues and choices. But the starting point is that we're still going to have reindeer in Norway, and all good forces must now be united in order for this to be viable and an important and positive element in our society.

But we must not regulate the whole industry, but let it be a nourishment with its rich cultural traditions that must have framework conditions and conditions that enable it to survive and live on its animals, without jeopardizing the nourishment of others or the quality of life of others.

The new reindeer management law was adopted in June 2007 and a significant change is that the new law has introduced a new scheme for determining the reindeer number. The rules of use shall determine the figures for each side according to the area of the site. The clean figure thus gets a direct anchoring in the accessible area at side level. This cut-off number shall be approved by the Purity Management Board. Prior to work on rules of use and renumbering, criteria were developed for determining reindeer numbers in cooperation with industry. These criteria shall be applied at all levels of the decision-making process. The new reindeer management law will be part of and responsible for the reindeer industry, and it will involve industry even to a much greater extent than before. What figures will be provided for each page will be established in 2010. Most of the pages have now come with their estimates, and then it is up to the board to approve them. Those who don't make their own calculations will get a calculation from the board. The process set up here provides, in the view of the Centre Party, the basis for a pasturage that will be sustainable over time. Once this process has been completed, it is assumed, of course, that the pure number will be reduced where it is too high in relation to what will be determined. I also show that work has been started on a major communication on agricultural policy, which will be presented in 2011. The Council of State confirmed in its statement that reindeer management will also have a place in this Communication and that a broad and thorough review of the reindeer management policy will be carried out to see if it is necessary to adjust the price or to take new action. The centre party insists that we continue to have viable reindeer farms in Norway, and I look forward to a good debate in the UK on the main issues and road choices when dealing with the agricultural notification.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e2412	First, I would like to thank the representative of the Skei Grande for the interpellation. As Minister of Agriculture and Food responsible for Animal Welfare, I am concerned that animals should feel good. From a welfare perspective, Norwegian animal husbandry issues were addressed in detail through the work on the new Animal Welfare Act that came into force from the turn of the year. The investigation – the background to the legal work – has already started with the work done in connection with St.meld. No 12 for 2002–2003, on animal husbandry and welfare. The Storing Communication identified a number of areas where further investigations related to different types of animal husbandry were needed, from a welfare perspective. A number of necessary investigations have thus been carried out in the context of the drafting of the new Animal Welfare Act. This included, inter alia: studies of the Scientific Committee on Food Safety, of the Veterinary Mission Centre, of SINTEF and of the University of Environmental and Life Sciences. The new law is intended to take account of new knowledge that has to come, and transform it into appropriate and good legislation for keeping animals. I therefore have great expectations that the law will be a good tool to promote animal welfare. The law acknowledges that animals have a self-esteem, in addition to having value for human beings. There is a requirement for competence for all keepers of animals. Food surveillance is working on the development of regulations to clarify such requirements of competence. It is also important to point out that there is now a general obligation to notify the law, so as to facilitate the collection of lack of supervision and care, thereby preventing the development of animals. Animal Tragics are often associated with disease and personal problems in keepers, as the representative Skei Grande also stressed. In this context, health professionals have been given the right to be warned, so that it is possible to notify of possible animal diseases without conflicting with the obligation of professional secrecy on which they are normally bound. The animal welfare regulations will be updated as new knowledge of animal needs is established and factors reflecting their well-being. The setting of requirements for the keeping of domestic animals will require a balance to be made of what results in satisfactory animal welfare, while at the same time the framework must be such that it is possible to implement it in practice. In recent times, there has been a strong focus on fur husbandry, after uncovering unsustainable conditions in parts of the industry. I've taken this into account, and I've made it clear to the industry that if they don't clean up, the political support is at stake. I have also had several meetings with the fur animal nutrition, and let me be informed of the work done, and the follow-up they are planning to improve and follow up the animal welfare of the country's fur animal husbandry. I have made it clear that the industry is not fresh until it tolerates a critical look at its business every day. The main responsibility/defence line for animal welfare is in the individual plant, in the individual fur breeder – we must never make any exception to this. I believe that the industry will do its best to follow up on this. The Animal Welfare Communication also stated that fur nutrition faces a number of challenges that need to be addressed before the industry can appear to be generally accepted. The welfare of fur animals needs to be significantly improved in the future, compared to the current forms of operation. Following this message, the Food Safety Authority has been entrusted with the task of preparing a draft new regulation on the keeping of fur animals. In the mandate given to the Food Safety Authority, it was said that the regulatory proposal had to contain all relevant aspects in order to achieve satisfactory animal welfare in line with new animal welfare laws. It was also clarified that the work had to be based on the fact that fur industry is a district industry that is desirable to maintain. A draft new regulation is currently being consulted. The draft has taken up many of the advice developed by the Scientific Committee on Food Safety, together with other technical and scientific material. This is in line with the leaders of the new Animal Welfare Act, building on current expertise and taking into account new knowledge. The proposed regulation facilitates the substantial improvement of animal welfare for fur animals. At the same time, the proposed changes will entail additional costs and additional work for the industry. I am determined that we will have an up-to-date legislation to improve the conditions for fur animals, and I will address the specific design of the legislation after the consultation. As regards the supervision of animal welfare legislation, responsibility for this has been added to the Food Safety Authority. The animal protection subjects are maintained in order to protect the human body, give local anchoring and legitimacy to the work on animal protection. From now on, the topics are also organised as part of the Food Authority. This organisation is selected for a clean and efficient system. There is therefore no ambiguity today related to the responsibility of the authorities. The monitoring of animal welfare is a priority task for the Food Authority, and the Food Authority has also been given more tools in the new Animal Welfare Act to effectively monitor breaches of the legislation. The answer to the inter-pellant Skei Grande's question is therefore that I do not see today a need to restart a critical review of all animal husbandry in Norway. This is something which, in line with the new Animal Welfare Act, will be assessed each time it becomes relevant to change existing legislation, or to develop new legislation, in the light of new knowledge. The government is concerned with animal welfare and, through its extensive work on the new Animal Welfare Act, has facilitated the proper and sound treatment of animals. The new law provides a good basis for further work to ensure that animal husbandry is carried out within a sound framework, based on knowledge and knowledge of the animal’s behavioural needs.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e2641	The night until Saturday 9 September 2000, Tommy Buskerud from Rome in Akershus was hit by an incident that was going to ruin his life. On the way home from Barachi's pub on the Klöfta, the 28-year-old Busherud is brutally defeated by two young people at the age of 19 and 22. A 21-year-old stabs him in the back with a 25-cm-long kitchen knife right up to the handle of butter. Lying on the ground, Busherud was repeatedly brutally kicked into his head before the youth leaves him at the edge of the ditch, helpless and bleeding. A few hours later he was discovered lying in a roundabout of a randomly passing driver. This is one of many, very tragic examples of blind violence that we can read about in the newspapers every day. Very many in our society are subjected to severe violence, which in most cases comes as lightning from the clear sky. It also happened in the example that I've shown here. A violent episode like this completely changes people's lives and causes most people to have major social and economic problems. Many people feel that they are unable to follow up the work, and family life is breaking up. Doers are often young and totally unable to make up for themselves. In addition, the level of penalties for such crimes in Norway is too low. The result is that the victim of violence is left with the bill, that is. a loss of life and economic ruin. In the case of Tommy Buskerud, the perpetrator who drove the knife in his back was punished with nine months of imprisonment and sentenced to pay 30,000 kroner in retribution. The other two perpetrators were both sentenced to 15 days of conditional prison and fines of NOK 3000. I think most people in society would think that this is completely unreasonable. At least I do, and I also hope that the State Council of Storberget is of the same opinion. Today, Buskerud is divorced and a hundred percent unemployed. Daily he takes seven to eight prescription medications against severe pain in order to be able to get out of bed at all. Often he keeps the bed several days in a stretch. There are many issues that you can focus on in an interpellation like this, the themes are very many, but I want to focus on the need to have a liberal compensation for violence victims, and that we have flexibility in the system, so that it captures everyone who has been exposed to innocent violence. Tommy Buskerud has also become a victim of the rule of lawfulness of society. Because the attack occurred in 2000, he did not come under the change that occurred in the victim's compensation from 1 July 2001, where the highest compensation is 20 times the basic amount of the popular benefit. He has to deal with an amount limited to NOK 200000, while those who were subjected to violence one year later can get compensation up to SEK 1.4 million. kr. Should the compensation be measured according to the situation and the legislation in force at the time when the case is completed? My answer to this is yes. Victims of violence must wait a long time for compensation to be paid, for example. because it takes a long time to bring the case before the courts and to have the application finalised by the Office for the Compensation of Victims of Violence. In addition, it is a major weakness that the amount of compensation is per person. Case and not for every victim of violence. This contributes to discrimination against victims of violence, as the Progress Party has pointed out in a note under the Ministry of Justice's budget for the current year. Many victims of violence have to spend large amounts in advance and put themselves in debt in order to obtain funds for the necessary medicines and treatment. Income is lost because you can no longer work and go on social welfare until you receive an application for disability benefit. The upliftment that comes from overwhelms and the public does not cover the expenses that have been incurred and those that will be paid in the future. The victim of violence is and will be the loser in the system. The perpetrator often escapes only with some scrapers in the form of replacement and low prison sentences. We know that the treatment of these matters will take many years. It is therefore necessary to ensure that the procedure can be speeded up and that the public services involved in dealing with these cases operate in an optimal manner. We have faced a situation of constant changes in the rules of the system of compensation for victims of violence by treating victims very differently, and it is unfortunate in a society where non-discrimination is otherwise central. I believe it is positive and correct that the level of compensation for victims has increased significantly in recent years, but we can ask ourselves whether the compensation is still at the right level. Appreciating the price of a ruined life is a difficult task, but it is obvious that the level is still too low. In addition, the level of punishment and compensation must rise significantly, so that the perpetrators have to take a greater part of the responsibility for the demise committed. I am also informed that in many reports of violence, the police often fail to inform about the rights of the victim and about the offer made by the public to victims of violence, including the rights and opportunities to apply for compensation for victims of violence. I am aware that a guide booklet from the Office for the Compensation of Victims has been prepared and that the website of the Office for the Compensation of Victims and also non-governmental organisations in this area can be used. That's good, but we still have to realize that there's still not everyone in our society who has the same prerequisites for finding out what their rights are and the ability to demand them. We need to be aware that for people who suffer considerable damage, both physically and mentally, after violence, guidance and assistance are particularly important. I hope that the Council of State against the police, the health services and others who face victims of violence early will clarify the importance of these being given help and guidance to assert their rights. Although the compensation itself does not relieve children's difficult experiences or the deep and painful memories they have created, in our opinion it is nevertheless an important platform for children to have to deal with the situation and not least to get on with life in the best possible way. Like others, I would like to thank the interpellant for raising the topic for debate, and I would also like to thank the Council of State for the job he and his department have done to put this important theme into a system in a good way.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-11.ana.ud725e3177	I'm sorry it was late, but that's the timetable here on the house. In Denmark, there has been a long-standing conflict in Copenhagen between the MC environment, in the form of Hells Angels and its AK 81 support group, on the one hand and young people with a different ethnic or cultural background on the other. In 2008, it was registered approximately. 900 gang members, while in 2009 the figure was 1250. The shooting episodes between the bands have expanded from the Nørrebro district to other parts of Copenhagen. In 2008, there were more than 60 shooting episodes. Since 1 August 2008 and until 20 October last year, the police have registered 339 shooting episodes in Denmark. 109 of them have been related to the gang conflict, which has cost six lives, 59 have been wounded and 40 people related to the gang environment have been targeted by the police. In the second quarter of 2009, there were 22 shooting episodes where the police thought it was about settlement in the gang environment; in the third quarter, 21 were registered. In March 2009, the Danish government presented the so-called gang package. The band package provides for more stringent penalties and a tightening of the expulsion rules relating to infringements of the arms law. This package has also provided more money to increase the amount of police in the streets. Copenhagen police and Sjælland felt the need to increase their efforts against these gang wars and set up their own special unit. This unit shall strengthen the further investigation against those persons who, directly or indirectly, play a leading role in the conflicts. It is called the Task Force East and will cooperate closely with other police departments, including the police. with the police intelligence service. So this is the situation in Denmark. In Norway, it has previously been known that several security companies operate with a paramilitary structure and are responsible for illegal interception, monitoring, use of violence, threats and killing and are likely to support the training of MC club members in Norway. The head of the unit for organized crime in Kripos, Atle Roll-Matthiesen, has previously stated that this is the same thing as in foreign fraternal clubs. In 2007, he said to TV 2 that it is not yet possible to predict a gang war in Norway, but that it is known from the past that small, marginal events have triggered major conflicts. Therefore, there is reason to take what is now happening seriously, he said. This message has also been repeated later this year. The perception of these as a threat comes to a large number of enquiries from around the country. Kripos notes that there are criminal MC bands and sympathizers to them in more than 70 municipalities in Norway. With criminal MC clubs, the police mean so-called one-percent motorcycle clubs, where members define themselves as the one percent of the population that has resigned from society, with their own rules of play. This is among the most and best organised criminal networks in Norway, according to Kripos. Many members of motorcycle clubs are, of course, family fathers — and perhaps some mothers too — with honest jobs and motorcycles as a hobby. It may be far from being a support group for a direct support group. But among the supporters there are also members involved in crime – on a mission or on an order from the mother club. We recently saw that Danish Hells Angels have visited Arendal several times and become familiar with parts of the local MC environment. In January, a 32-year-old Danish citizen was held in custody for what the police believe is gross violence against one of the guests in a local pub. One 20-year-old man of the arendal must have had two teeth knocked out – and more injured other parts of the body. The police are linking the incident to Hells Angels and have proposed to deny members access to the symbols of the outside club. The police in Denmark believe that the gang war there started as a debate on the sale of drugs, but that it has evolved into other types of crime, such as trafficking in human beings, bribery and torpedo activities. To date, Norway has been proud of a comparable situation, but that is why – and I know that the Council of State does – to think preventively in view of the fact that our small, rich country can risk the same thing in the future and learn from the positive, negative experiences of the Danes. The Norwegian authorities responded in cash after NOKAS, but we are in arrears – everyone sees it – in relation to organised profit crime from Eastern Europe. Eastern European gangs have largely taken over the market from Norwegian ransacks, and that is a major threat. Those we know today have described themselves as a forerunner, like a huge swarm of criminals. They laugh at us and think we're fast. In the middle of this situation, it looks like a new police crisis is about to occur, with cuts in annual work compared to what was foreseen. We've been discussing the criminal justice system and the situation in it, and the courts are notifying manning stops. While the police have partially analysed the threat picture, but believe that we are in arrears, it has been decided to make the settlements considerably more modest for a prisoner composition that had not been assessed in advance in relation to the threat picture. There is no «quick fix» on this, and the Right asks the Government to initiate a broad settlement, so that society returns before it is too late and some of these groups start fighting for markets, possibly cooperating. If it is the case that the budget negotiations have been informed that money needs to be saved, it should not go beyond one of the country's basic tasks. We see that publishing tax lists, linking to new search engines, social media, etc. contribute to the promotion of economic, organised crime and ID thefts, both under the control of Eastern Europeans and possibly also under the control of MC bands. Yesterday, if I.D. insurance said that they were giving money from the NOKAS to the theft register. According to IF, this is one of the best means available to prevent the spread of stolen goods, whether they are generated by Eastern European gangs or also by MC bands.
